1 Maccabees 1:1

After Alexander son of Philip, the Macedonian who came from the land of Kittim, had defeated Darius king of the Persians and Medes, he became king in his place, having first ruled over Greece.

  1. 1 Maccabees 6:2 Narrative connection

    The later account explicitly recalls Alexander son of Philip as the Macedonian king who first ruled the Greeks, identifying the ruler introduced here.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 6:2 Its temple was exceedingly rich, and there were golden coverings, breastplates, and weapons left there by Alexander son of Philip, the Macedonian king who first reigned among the Greeks.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:10

From them came a sinful root: Antiochus Epiphanes, son of King Antiochus, who had been a hostage in Rome. He became king in the one hundred and thirty-seventh year of the kingdom of the Greeks.

  1. 2 Maccabees 4:7 Historical parallel

    Both accounts identify the accession of Antiochus called Epiphanes; 2 Maccabees places Jason's acquisition of the high priesthood at that transition.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 4:7 When Seleucus departed this life and Antiochus, called Epiphanes, took over the kingdom, Jason, Onias’s brother, obtained the high priesthood by fraud.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:11

In those days lawless sons came out of Israel and persuaded many, saying, “Let us go and make a covenant with the nations around us, because since we separated ourselves from them, many evils have overtaken us.”

  1. Exodus 23:32-33 Law and practice

    The proposed covenant with surrounding nations reverses the law's prohibition of covenants with them and its warning that their worship would ensnare Israel.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 23:32 You shall make no covenant with them, nor with their gods;

    Exodus 23:33 neither shall they dwell in your land, lest they cause you to sin against Me; for if you serve their gods, these shall be a stumbling block to you.”

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:12

The proposal was good in their eyes.

  1. 2 Maccabees 4:10-11 Historical parallel

    The favourable reception of foreign customs corresponds to Jason's successful introduction of the Greek manner of life and displacement of lawful civic practices.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 4:10 When the king consented and Jason gained office, he immediately turned his fellow countrymen toward the Greek manner of life.

    2 Maccabees 4:11 He set aside the benevolent royal concessions established for the Jews through John, the father of Eupolemus, who had undertaken the embassy to the Romans for friendship and alliance. Abolishing the lawful forms of civic life, he introduced unlawful customs.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:13

Some of the people eagerly went to the king, and he gave them authority to follow the ordinances of the nations.

  1. 2 Maccabees 4:9-11 Historical parallel

    Both accounts connect the adoption of Greek institutions with royal permission, while 2 Maccabees names Jason and specifies his financial proposal.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 4:9 Beyond these he promised to pay a further one hundred fifty if he were permitted by royal authority to establish a gymnasium and a training institution for young men, and to enroll the people in Jerusalem as Antiochenes.

    2 Maccabees 4:10 When the king consented and Jason gained office, he immediately turned his fellow countrymen toward the Greek manner of life.

    2 Maccabees 4:11 He set aside the benevolent royal concessions established for the Jews through John, the father of Eupolemus, who had undertaken the embassy to the Romans for friendship and alliance. Abolishing the lawful forms of civic life, he introduced unlawful customs.

1 Maccabees 1:14

They built a gymnasium in Jerusalem according to the customs of the nations

  1. 2 Maccabees 4:12 Historical parallel

    Both accounts describe the establishment of a gymnasium in Jerusalem; 2 Maccabees locates it beneath the citadel and names Jason as its sponsor.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 4:12 For he eagerly established a gymnasium immediately beneath the citadel and brought the most distinguished young men under his direction to wear the broad-brimmed hat.

1 Maccabees 1:15

and made foreskins for themselves. They abandoned the holy covenant, yoked themselves to the nations, and sold themselves to do evil.

  1. Genesis 17:10-14 Law and practice

    The reversal of circumcision is described as abandonment of the covenant; Genesis identifies circumcision in the flesh as its enduring sign.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 17:10 And this is the covenant that you shall keep, between Me and you, and your seed after you throughout their generations: every male among you shall be circumcised.

    Genesis 17:11 And you shall be circumcised in the flesh of your foreskin; and it shall be a sign of the covenant between Me and you.

    Genesis 17:12 And every male child among you, eight days old, shall be circumcised throughout your generations, the slave born in your house, and the slave bought with silver from any foreigner who is not of your seed.

    Genesis 17:13 The slave born in your house, and the slave bought with silver, shall surely be circumcised; and My covenant shall be upon your flesh as an everlasting covenant.

    Genesis 17:14 And the uncircumcised male who is not circumcised in the flesh of his foreskin on the eighth day, that soul shall be destroyed from his kindred; for he has broken My covenant.”

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Maccabees 4:14-16 Historical parallel

    The abandonment of ancestral covenantal identity corresponds to priests neglecting the altar and preferring Greek distinctions in the companion account.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 4:14 that the priests were no longer eager for the services of the altar. Despising the temple and neglecting the sacrifices, they hurried to take part in the unlawful provision of exercises in the wrestling ground after the signal for the discus.

    2 Maccabees 4:15 They regarded their ancestral honours as nothing and considered Greek distinctions the finest.

    2 Maccabees 4:16 For this reason a difficult crisis overtook them: those whose ways they admired and whom they wished to resemble in everything became their enemies and punishers.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:16

The kingdom was established before Antiochus, and he planned to reign over the land of Egypt so that he might rule both kingdoms.

  1. 2 Maccabees 5:1 Historical parallel

    Both accounts report Antiochus campaigning in Egypt; 2 Maccabees explicitly calls its expedition his second, so the two chronological presentations are not collapsed.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 5:1 About this time Antiochus organized his second expedition into Egypt.

    Both accounts connect Antiochus's movement from Egypt with an armed assault on Jerusalem; 2 Maccabees adds the report of revolt as his motive.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 5:11 When news of these events reached the king, he concluded that Judea was in revolt. He therefore broke camp in Egypt, savage in spirit, took the city by force of arms,

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 5:16 With defiled hands he took the sacred vessels, and with profane hands swept away the gifts other kings had dedicated for the enhancement, glory and honour of the place.

1 Maccabees 1:29

After two full years, the king sent a chief collector of tribute to the cities of Judah. He came to Jerusalem with a heavy force.

  1. 2 Maccabees 5:24-26 Historical comparison

    Both narratives describe a royal agent arriving with troops before a deceptive attack on Jerusalem; the titles and troop details differ, so this comparison does not equate the unnamed tribute collector with Apollonius.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 5:24 he sent Apollonius, commander of the Mysians, with an army of twenty-two thousand, ordering him to slaughter all the men in their prime and sell the women and the younger people.

    2 Maccabees 5:25 On arriving in Jerusalem, Apollonius pretended to be peaceable and waited until the holy day of the Sabbath. Finding the Jews at rest, he ordered those under him to parade under arms.

    2 Maccabees 5:26 He stabbed all who came out to watch, and rushing armed into the city, struck down considerable multitudes.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:30

He spoke peaceful words to them deceitfully, and they trusted him. Then he suddenly fell upon the city, struck it a great blow, and destroyed many people of Israel.

  1. 2 Maccabees 5:25-26 Historical comparison

    Both narratives describe a commander feigning peace in Jerusalem before suddenly attacking its trusting inhabitants; 2 Maccabees adds the Sabbath setting. The unnamed tribute collector is not identified with Apollonius here.

1 Maccabees 1:38

Because of them the inhabitants of Jerusalem fled, and she became a dwelling of foreigners. She became foreign to those born in her, and her children abandoned her.

  1. 1 Maccabees 3:45 Narrative connection

    The later description of Jerusalem as deserted by its native inhabitants and occupied by foreigners resumes the displacement reported here.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 3:45 Jerusalem was uninhabited like a wilderness. None of those born in her went in or out; the sanctuary was being trampled, and foreigners were in the citadel, a lodging for the nations. Delight was removed from Jacob, and flute and lyre ceased.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:39

Her sanctuary was laid waste like a wilderness; her festivals were turned into mourning, her sabbaths into reproach, and her honour into contempt.

  1. 2 Maccabees 6:6 Historical parallel

    The transformation of Sabbaths and festivals into occasions of reproach corresponds to the companion account's prohibition of Sabbath keeping and ancestral festivals.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 6:6 It was impossible to keep the Sabbath, observe ancestral festivals, or even profess simply to be a Jew.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 1:40

In proportion to her glory, her dishonour multiplied, and her exaltation was turned into mourning.

    Within the book, the city's honour turning into shame is reversed by the rededication's great gladness and removal of the nations' reproach.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 4:58 There was exceedingly great gladness among the people, and the reproach of the nations was turned away.

1 Maccabees 1:41

The king wrote to his whole kingdom that all should become one people

  1. Daniel 3:4-7 Thematic comparison

    Antiochus's demand for uniformity across his kingdom resembles Nebuchadnezzar's universal summons to common worship; these are distinct rulers and events.

    Read matching passage

    Daniel 3:4 The herald proclaimed to the crowds, “You are commanded, nations and countries, peoples and languages:

    Daniel 3:5 when you hear the sound of trumpet, panpipe, kithara, sambuca, psaltery, symphonia, and every kind of musical instrument, fall down and bow to the golden image that King Nebuchadnezzar has set up.

    Daniel 3:6 They will throw into the blazing furnace anyone who does not fall down and bow.”

    Daniel 3:7 At that time, when all the nations heard the sound of the trumpet and every musical sound, all the nations, tribes, and languages fell down and bowed to the golden image that Nebuchadnezzar had set up before him.

    Pinned source record

and that each should abandon his own customs. All the nations accepted the king’s command.

    2 Maccabees 6:1 Not long afterward the king sent an elderly Athenian to compel the Jews to depart from their ancestral laws and cease conducting their lives according to God’s laws,

1 Maccabees 1:43

Many from Israel consented to his worship, sacrificed to idols, and profaned the sabbath.

  1. Exodus 20:8-11 Law and practice

    The Israelites' profanation of the Sabbath violates the command to remember and sanctify the seventh day.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 20:8 Remember the Sabbath day, to keep it holy.

    Exodus 20:9 For six days you shall labour and do all your works;

    Exodus 20:10 but the seventh day is a Sabbath to the Lord your God. You shall do no work on it: you, and your son, and your daughter, your male slave, and your female slave, your ox, and your beast of burden, and all your livestock, and the resident foreigner who dwells with you.

    Exodus 20:11 For in six days the Lord made heaven and earth and the sea and everything in them, and rested on the seventh day. Therefore the Lord blessed the seventh day and made it holy.

    Pinned source record

to prevent whole burnt offerings, sacrifice, and libation in the sanctuary, to profane sabbaths and festivals,

  2. Genesis 22:1-12 Explicit ancestral reference

    The reference to Abraham being found faithful in testing recalls God's test involving Isaac and the recognition that Abraham feared God.

    Read matching passage

    Genesis 22:1 And after these things God tested Abraham, and said to him, “Abraham, Abraham.” And he said, “Behold, here am I.”

    Genesis 22:2 And He said, “Take your son, your beloved, whom you have loved, Isaac, and go to the high land; and offer him there for a whole burnt offering upon one of the mountains which I shall tell you.”

    Genesis 22:3 And Abraham rose in the morning, and saddled his female donkey, and took with him two servants and Isaac his son. And he split wood for the whole burnt offering, and arose and went, and came to the place of which God had spoken to him.

    Genesis 22:4 On the third day Abraham lifted up his eyes, and saw the place far off.

    Genesis 22:5 And Abraham said to his servants, “Stay here with the female donkey; and I and the lad will go over there, and worship, and return to you.”

    Genesis 22:6 And Abraham took the wood of the whole burnt offering, and laid it upon Isaac his son; and he took the fire in his hand, and the knife. And the two went together.

    Genesis 22:7 And Isaac spoke to Abraham his father, and said, “Father.” And he said, “What is it, child?” And he said, “Behold the fire and the wood; but where is the sheep for the whole burnt offering?”

    Genesis 22:8 And Abraham said, “God will provide for Himself a sheep for the whole burnt offering, child.” And the two went together,

    Genesis 22:9 and came to the place of which God had spoken to him. And Abraham built an altar there, and laid the wood upon it, and bound together the feet of Isaac his son, and laid him upon the altar, upon the wood.

    Genesis 22:10 And Abraham stretched out his hand to take the knife, to slaughter his son.

    Genesis 22:11 And an angel of the Lord called to him out of the heaven, and said to him, “Abraham, Abraham.” And he said, “Behold, here am I.”

    Genesis 22:12 And he said, “Do not lay your hand upon the lad or do anything to him; for now I know that you fear God, and have not spared your beloved son for my sake.”

  1. 2 Maccabees 10:1-3 Historical parallel

    The resolve to cleanse and rededicate the holy places corresponds to the companion account of Judas recovering and purifying the temple.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 10:1 Maccabeus and those with him, the Lord leading them, recovered the temple and the city.

    2 Maccabees 10:2 They tore down the altars built by the foreigners in the marketplace, and also the sacred precincts.

    2 Maccabees 10:3 Having purified the temple, they made another altar. They heated stones, took fire from them, and offered sacrifices after a period of two years. They offered incense, lit lamps and set out the loaves.

    Pinned source record

1 Maccabees 4:38

  1. 1 Maccabees 1:37-39 Narrative connection

    The condition Judas's army sees at the sanctuary confirms the earlier report of its defilement and wilderness-like desolation.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 1:37 They poured out innocent blood around the sanctuary and defiled the sanctuary.

    1 Maccabees 1:38 Because of them the inhabitants of Jerusalem fled, and she became a dwelling of foreigners. She became foreign to those born in her, and her children abandoned her.

    1 Maccabees 1:39 Her sanctuary was laid waste like a wilderness; her festivals were turned into mourning, her sabbaths into reproach, and her honour into contempt.

1 Maccabees 4:47

They took whole stones according to the law and built a new altar like the former one.

  1. Deuteronomy 27:5-6 Explicit legal reference

    The use of whole stones according to the law directly corresponds to the instruction to build an altar of whole stones untouched by iron.

    Read matching passage

    Deuteronomy 27:5 And you shall build there an altar unto the Lord your God, an altar of stones; you shall lay no iron upon them.

    Deuteronomy 27:6 You shall build an altar of whole stones unto the Lord your God, and you shall offer thereon whole burnt offerings unto the Lord your God.

    Pinned source record

  2. Exodus 20:25 Law and practice

    The requirement to use whole stones is reinforced by the prohibition of hewn altar stones, because a blade would defile them.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 20:25 But if you make Me an altar of stones, you shall not build with hewn stones; for you have laid your blade upon them, and they are defiled.

    Leviticus 24:4 You shall burn the lamps upon the pure lampstand before the Lord until morning.

  1. Leviticus 24:5-8 Law and practice

    The return of loaves to the table restores the prescribed arrangement of bread before the Lord.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 24:5 And you shall take fine flour and make twelve loaves from it. Each loaf shall contain two tenths.

    Leviticus 24:6 And you shall place them in two arrangements, six in each arrangement, on the pure table before the Lord.

    Leviticus 24:7 And you shall put pure frankincense and salt upon the arrangement. They shall be set before the Lord as a memorial for the loaves.

    Leviticus 24:8 On the Sabbath day he shall set it before the Lord continually, before the sons of Israel as an everlasting covenant.

1 Maccabees 5:2

They resolved to remove the descendants of Jacob living among them, and began killing and destroying people.

  1. 2 Maccabees 12:3-8 Historical comparison

    Both accounts describe attacks on Jewish communities living among other peoples; the Joppa killings and Jamnia plot illustrate the same danger without identifying them with all the attacks summarized here.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 12:3 The people of Joppa committed this great impiety. They invited the Jews living among them to board boats that they had provided, with their wives and children, as though there were no hostility toward them,

    2 Maccabees 12:4 in accordance with the city’s common decree. The Jews accepted, wishing to live peaceably and suspecting nothing; but the people took them out to sea and drowned no fewer than two hundred.

    2 Maccabees 12:5 When Judas learned of the cruelty committed against his fellow countrymen, he gave orders to the men with him.

    2 Maccabees 12:6 Calling upon God, the righteous Judge, he came against his brothers’ murderers. By night he burned the harbour and set the boats ablaze, and stabbed those who had fled there.

    2 Maccabees 12:7 Since the town was shut, he withdrew, intending to return and uproot the whole community of Joppa.

    2 Maccabees 12:8 Learning also that the people of Jamnia intended to act in the same way toward the Jews living among them,

1 Maccabees 5:3

Judas fought against the sons of Esau in Idumea, in Akrabattene, because they were besieging Israel. He struck them a great blow, subdued them, and took their spoils.

  1. 2 Maccabees 10:15-17 Historical comparison

    Both accounts present Judas attacking Idumeans who were oppressing Israel; the source names Akrabattene, while the target describes assaults on fortresses and gives a casualty total. The differing campaign settings remain distinct.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 10:15 Along with him, the Idumeans, who held strategic fortresses, harassed the Jews. Taking in those driven from Jerusalem, they attempted to maintain the war.

    2 Maccabees 10:16 Maccabeus and his men offered an entreaty and asked God to become their ally; then they rushed against the Idumean fortresses.

    2 Maccabees 10:17 Attacking them vigorously, they gained control of the places, drove off all who fought on the wall, and slaughtered those they encountered. They killed no fewer than twenty thousand.

1 Maccabees 5:5

He shut them into the towers, encamped against them, devoted them to destruction, and burned the towers with everyone inside.

  1. 2 Maccabees 10:18-23 Historical comparison

    Both accounts describe hostile groups sheltering in towers that Maccabeus captures with lethal force; the sons of Baean are not identified with the unnamed occupants of the two Idumean towers.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 10:18 No fewer than nine thousand fled together into two very strong towers, which had everything necessary to withstand a siege.

    2 Maccabees 10:19 Maccabeus left Simon and Joseph, and also Zacchaeus with his men, a sufficient force to besiege them, and himself departed for places requiring urgent attention.

    2 Maccabees 10:20 But Simon’s men, in their love of money, were persuaded with silver by some of those in the towers. Receiving seventy thousand drachmas, they let some slip away.

    2 Maccabees 10:21 When Maccabeus was informed of what had happened, he gathered the people’s leaders and accused them of selling their brothers for money by releasing enemies against them.

    2 Maccabees 10:22 He therefore put these men to death as traitors and immediately captured the two towers.

    2 Maccabees 10:23 Succeeding in everything by the weapons in his hands, he destroyed more than twenty thousand in the two fortresses.

  1. 1 Maccabees 1:1 Narrative identification

    The temple weapons are attributed to Alexander son of Philip, the Macedonian first ruler of the Greeks, identifying the conqueror introduced at the book's opening.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 1:1 After Alexander son of Philip, the Macedonian who came from the land of Kittim, had defeated Darius king of the Persians and Medes, he became king in his place, having first ruled over Greece.

He came and tried to seize the city and plunder it, but could not, because the people of the city learned of his plan.

  1. 2 Maccabees 9:2 Historical comparison

    Both accounts describe Antiochus attempting to plunder a city's temple and being resisted by its inhabitants; their city names differ.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 9:2 For he had entered the city called Persepolis and attempted to rob its temple and oppress the city. The multitudes therefore rushed to defend themselves with weapons, and his men were routed. Antiochus, put to flight by the inhabitants, had to make a disgraceful retreat.

    Pinned source record

    1 Maccabees 4:30 He saw the strong army and prayed, saying, “Blessed art Thou, Saviour of Israel, who didst crush the mighty man’s assault by the hand of Thy servant David, and didst deliver the foreigners’ army into the hands of Jonathan son of Saul and his armour-bearer.

    1 Maccabees 4:31 So shut this army into the hand of Thy people Israel, and let them be ashamed of their force and their cavalry.

    1 Maccabees 4:32 Give them cowardice; melt the boldness of their strength, and let them be shaken by their collapse.

    1 Maccabees 4:33 Bring them down by the sword of those who love Thee, and let all who know Thy name praise Thee with hymns.”

    1 Maccabees 4:34 They joined battle with one another. About five thousand men from Lysias’s army fell; they fell before them.

    1 Maccabees 4:35 Lysias saw the rout of his own formation, the courage that had arisen in Judas’s men, and how they were ready either to live or to die nobly. He withdrew to Antioch and recruited mercenaries in greater numbers to come again into Judea.

    Both portray Antiochus becoming gravely ill after his reverses, but this text stresses sorrow and frustrated plans while the other describes bodily affliction as divine judgment.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 9:6 most justly, since he had tormented the bowels of others with many strange afflictions.

    2 Maccabees 9:7 Yet he did not cease his haughtiness at all, but was still filled with arrogance, breathing fire in his anger against the Jews and ordering the journey hastened. He happened to fall from the chariot as it rushed along, and in the severe fall all the members of his body were wrenched.

    2 Maccabees 9:8 He who just now, in arrogance beyond human measure, thought he could command the waves of the sea and imagined he could weigh the mountain heights in a balance, was brought down to the ground and carried on a litter, plainly showing God’s power to everyone.

    2 Maccabees 9:9 Worms swarmed up from the impious man’s body, and while he was still alive in pain and anguish, his flesh fell away. The whole army was burdened by the stench of his decay.

    2 Maccabees 9:10 No one could carry the man who a little earlier thought he could touch the stars of heaven, because of the unbearable weight of his smell.

    2 Maccabees 9:11 Then, broken, he began to cease much of his arrogance and come to understanding, his pains increasing every moment under the divine scourge.

1 Maccabees 6:49

He made peace with the people of Beth-zur, and they came out of the city, because they had no provisions there to sustain a siege, since it was a sabbath for the land.

  1. Leviticus 25:3-7 Law and practice

    Beth-zur's shortage during the land's sabbath is situated within the law requiring the seventh year to be a rest from sowing, pruning, and ordinary harvest.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 25:3 For six years you shall sow your field, and for six years you shall prune your vine and gather its fruit.

    Leviticus 25:4 But in the seventh year the land shall have a Sabbath, a rest, a Sabbath unto the Lord. You shall not sow your field or prune your vine.

    Leviticus 25:5 And you shall not reap the things growing of themselves in your field or gather the grapes of your consecration. It shall be a year of rest for the land.

    Leviticus 25:6 And the land’s Sabbath produce shall be food for you, your male slave, your female slave, your hired worker, and the lodger attached to you.

    Leviticus 25:7 And all its produce shall also be food for your livestock and the wild animals in your land.

    Pinned source record

  2. 2 Maccabees 13:22 Historical comparison

    Both describe a negotiated settlement at Beth-zur; this account explains the surrender through lack of provisions in the sabbatical year, whereas the other emphasizes exchanged pledges and withdrawal.

    Read matching passage

    2 Maccabees 13:22 The king negotiated a second time with those in Beth-zur, gave a pledge, received one, and went away. He attacked Judas’s men and was defeated.

    Pinned source record

  1. Exodus 23:10-11 Law and practice

    The notice that it was the seventh year invokes the agricultural cycle in which the land is left to rest after six years of cultivation, helping explain the depleted stores.

    Read matching passage

    Exodus 23:10 For six years you shall sow your land, and gather its produce;

    Exodus 23:11 but in the seventh you shall grant it release, and leave it; and the poor of your nation shall eat, and what they leave the wild beasts shall eat. You shall do the same with your vineyard and your olive grove.

  1. 1 Maccabees 1:41-50 Narrative reversal

    Restoration of the Jews' ancestral customs reverses Antiochus's earlier decrees forbidding their laws, sacrifices, Sabbaths, and circumcision, the very policy now acknowledged as the cause of revolt.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 1:41 The king wrote to his whole kingdom that all should become one people

    1 Maccabees 1:43 Many from Israel consented to his worship, sacrificed to idols, and profaned the sabbath.

    1 Maccabees 1:44 The king sent letters by messengers to Jerusalem and the cities of Judah, ordering them to follow customs foreign to the land,

    1 Maccabees 1:45 to prevent whole burnt offerings, sacrifice, and libation in the sanctuary, to profane sabbaths and festivals,

    1 Maccabees 1:46 to defile the sanctuary and holy people,

    1 Maccabees 1:47 to build pagan altars, sacred precincts, and shrines for idols, and to sacrifice pigs and unclean livestock,

    1 Maccabees 1:48 to leave their sons uncircumcised, and to make themselves abominable through every uncleanness and profanation,

    1 Maccabees 1:49 so that they would forget the law and change all the ordinances.

    1 Maccabees 1:50 Whoever did not act according to the king’s command was to die.

  1. Leviticus 23:34-43 Calendar and practice

    The seventh-month Festival of Booths at which Jonathan dons the holy robe corresponds to the law's seventh-month Feast of Tents; the law supplies the festival calendar, not authorization for Alexander's priestly appointment.

    Read matching passage

    Leviticus 23:34 Speak to the sons of Israel, saying: On the fifteenth day of this seventh month is the Feast of Tents unto the Lord for seven days.

    Leviticus 23:35 And on the first day there shall be a holy convocation. You shall do no laborious work.

    Leviticus 23:36 For seven days you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. The eighth day shall be a holy convocation for you, and you shall bring whole burnt offerings unto the Lord. It is a closing observance; you shall do no laborious work.

    Leviticus 23:37 These are the feasts for the Lord, which you shall proclaim as holy convocations, to present offerings unto the Lord: whole burnt offerings and their sacrifices and their drink offerings, each day’s requirement on its day,

    Leviticus 23:38 besides the Lord’s Sabbaths, besides your gifts, besides all your vows, and besides your freewill offerings that you give unto the Lord.

    Leviticus 23:39 And on the fifteenth day of this seventh month, when you finish gathering the produce of the land, you shall celebrate unto the Lord for seven days. On the first day there shall be rest, and on the eighth day there shall be rest.

    Leviticus 23:40 And on the first day you shall take beautiful fruit of a tree, and palm fronds, and dense branches of a tree, and willows, and branches of the chaste tree from a stream, to rejoice before the Lord your God seven days

    Leviticus 23:41 of the year. This is an everlasting ordinance throughout your generations; you shall celebrate it in the seventh month.

    Leviticus 23:42 You shall dwell in tents seven days; every native in Israel shall dwell in tents,

    Leviticus 23:43 so that your generations may see that I made the sons of Israel dwell in tents when I brought them out of the land of Egypt. I am the Lord your God.

  2. 1 Samuel 5:2-5 Thematic comparison

    Both narratives portray humiliation of Dagon's cult in its sanctuary, here by Jonathan's burning and there by the idol falling before the ark; Azotus and Ashdod are kept as the texts name them, and identity of the buildings is not asserted.

1 Maccabees 16:10

They fled into the towers in the countryside of Azotus, and he burned it with fire. About two thousand of their men fell. He returned to Judea in peace.

  1. 1 Maccabees 10:83-85 Azotus retreat compared

    John's defeated enemies retreat into towers in Azotus's countryside before he burns 'it'; the earlier campaign also ends with flight to Azotus and burning, but explicitly names the city and Dagon's shrine. The refuges and casualty totals differ; the source's pronoun does not explicitly identify the towers as what burned.

    Read matching passage

    1 Maccabees 10:83 and the cavalry scattered in the plain. They fled into Azotus and entered Beth-dagon, their idol shrine, for safety.

    1 Maccabees 10:84 Jonathan burned Azotus and its surrounding towns and took their spoils. He burned the temple of Dagon and those who had fled into it with fire.

    1 Maccabees 10:85 Those who had fallen by the sword, together with those burned, numbered about eight thousand men.
